Higher thresholds ($\\geq 1.7\\times$) sacrifice recall without eliminating the 008e\\_v3\\_s2 false positive (which requires $\\tau > 3.78$).","+\\item \\textbf{Perfect classification.} The $1.5\\times$ threshold achieves 100\\% accuracy on the $n{=}11$ factorial set: all four CIVO cases (006, 059, 008d, 009b) are detected, and no non-CIVO experiment exceeds the threshold (AUROC${=}1.000$, F1${=}100\\%$).","+\\item \\textbf{Maximum recall with margin.} The lowest CIVO-positive NVE ($1.62\\times$, 009b) clears the threshold by $0.12\\times$; the highest negative ($1.00\\times$, 001) sits $0.50\\times$ below it.","+\\item \\textbf{Robustness.} The $1.5\\times$ threshold achieves perfect classification across the extended $n{=}17$ two-environment evaluation (Table~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2_full}), with the nearest positive--negative gap widening further ($1.53\\times$ vs.\\ $1.04\\times$)."," \\end{enumerate}"," ","-For Task~B1 (sustained CIVO only), the optimal threshold $\\nve \\geq 3.9\\times$ achieves $10/11$ accuracy ($90.9\\%$, 95\\% CI: [58.7\\%, 99.8\\%]); the sole false positive is exp\\_059 (NVE${=}4.72\\times$, self-correcting CIVO classified as B1-negative).","+For Task~B1 (sustained CIVO only), the optimal threshold $\\nve \\geq 1.7\\times$ achieves $10/11$ accuracy ($90.9\\%$, 95\\% CI: [58.7\\%, 99.8\\%]); the sole false positive is exp\\_059 (NVE${=}4.72\\times$, self-correcting CIVO classified as B1-negative)."," "," \\subsection{Expanded ROC Analysis}"," \\label{sec:roc_analysis}"]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01U5gF6NNZX1Km5S1zb3n5Z3","type":"tool_result","content":"253\tThe original $n{=}17$ analysis (Tables~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b1}--\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2_full}) predates the SGS, RAVE, and TCN experiments. We extend the NVE diagnostic evaluation to $n{=}56$ configurations spanning all tested interventions. Figure~\\ref{fig:nve_roc} shows the receiver operating characteristic curve with 95\\% bootstrap confidence interval (10{,}000 resamples).\n254\t\n255\t\\begin{figure}[h]\n256\t\\centering\n257\t\\includegraphics[width=0.7\\columnwidth]{../../artifacts/figures/nve_roc_curve.pdf}\n258\t\\caption{NVE ROC curve ($n{=}56$ configurations, AUROC${=}0.990$, comprehensive validation). The preset $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$ operating point (star) achieves TPR${=}1.00$, FPR${=}0.09$, accuracy${=}53/56$, F1${=}0.941$. Three false positives arise from non-CIVO value overestimation: mitigation transient VE spike (008e\\_v3 subsample, $1.93\\times$) and frozen embedding transients (047, $1.71\\times$; 057, $1.88\\times$). Threshold calibrated and validated post-hoc; cross-architecture validation deferred.}\n259\t\\label{fig:nve_roc}\n260\t\\end{figure}\n261\t\n262\t\\begin{figure}[h]\n263\t\\centering\n264\t\\includegraphics[width=0.7\\columnwidth]{../../artifacts/figures/nve_threshold_prf.pdf}\n265\t\\caption{Precision, Recall, and F1 as a function of NVE threshold ($n{=}56$ configurations). Peak F1${=}0.941$ at $\\tau{=}1.5\\times$, where recall remains perfect ($1.00$) and precision${=}0.889$. The plateau of high F1 across $\\tau \\in [1.5, 1.7]$ indicates robustness to threshold selection.}\n266\t\\label{fig:nve_prf}\n267\t\\end{figure}\n268\t\n269\tWe label a configuration as CIVO-positive if and only if (i)~it uses a trained $\\dt$-embedding with multi-rate data exposure, and (ii)~NVE exceeds $1.5\\times$ with co-occurring entropy collapse. Configurations exhibiting high NVE through other mechanisms (gradient truncation side effects, value anchor pollution) are labeled non-CIVO.\n270\t\n271\tThe three false positives (FPR${=}0.18$) are mechanistically distinct from CIVO: (1)~RAVE anchor pollution (exp\\_028, the EMA anchor becomes inflated, producing overestimation through a different pathway); (2)~obs-decoder SGS-induced value distortion (exp\\_025 seed~2, blocking observation decoder gradients damages the RSSM latent space); (3)~IRD eval-driven collapse (exp\\_008e\\_v3 seed~2, actor entropy \\emph{increases} with evaluation collapse, mechanistically distinct from CIVO's entropy \\emph{decrease}). All three produce NVE $> 1.5\\times$ through non-CIVO mechanisms, indicating that the NVE diagnostic detects value overestimation broadly rather than CIVO specifically---a feature rather than a limitation for practitioners monitoring deployment health. The closest sub-threshold case (008f seed~4, NVE${=}1.41\\times$) lacks $\\dt$-conditioning entirely ($d{=}0$); its elevated NVE reflects transient value noise that self-corrects without the positive feedback loop characteristic of CIVO. The closest above-threshold positive (008d seed~3, NVE${=}1.53\\times$) exhibits the full CIVO causal chain: trained $\\dt$-embedding, entropy decrease, and evaluation collapse to 14\\% of baseline.\n272\t\n273\t\\paragraph{Comprehensive validation ($n{=}56$).}\n274\tExpanding the evaluation set to all classifiable experiments yields $n{=}56$ ($n_{\\text{pos}}{=}24$, $n_{\\text{neg}}{=}32$), spanning Walker Walk, Cheetah Run, and multiple intervention configurations (SGS, TCN, dual-critic, frozen embedding). At the $1.5\\times$ threshold: AUROC${=}0.990$, precision${=}0.889$, recall${=}1.000$, F1${=}0.941$, accuracy${=}53/56$. The three false positives are: exp\\_008e\\_v3\\_subsample ($1.93\\times$, mitigation config with transient VE spike), exp\\_047 ($1.71\\times$, frozen embedding transient), and exp\\_057 ($1.88\\times$, frozen embedding transient). All 24 CIVO-positive configurations are correctly detected (FN${=}0$), with CIVO-positive NVE ranging from $1.53\\times$ (008d seed~3, late-onset) to $70.64\\times$ (010b seed~0, Cheetah). The substantial increase in sample size from $n{=}32$ to $n{=}56$ strengthens confidence in the $1.5\\times$ threshold as a robust diagnostic boundary.\n275\t\n276\t\\subsection{NVE Normalization Robustness}\n277\t\\label{sec:nve_robustness}"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"a08170ba-d0ed-49ec-918a-a297ecfee54f","uuid":"d85cf9d7-c2ca-414e-aaee-3a6b9eabfa31","timestamp":"2026-05-25T16:36:37.517Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_9/projects/event-gated-adaptive-horizon/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex","content":"The original $n{=}17$ analysis (Tables~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b1}--\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2_full}) predates the SGS, RAVE, and TCN experiments. We extend the NVE diagnostic evaluation to $n{=}56$ configurations spanning all tested interventions. Figure~\\ref{fig:nve_roc} shows the receiver operating characteristic curve with 95\\% bootstrap confidence interval (10{,}000 resamples).\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.7\\columnwidth]{../../artifacts/figures/nve_roc_curve.pdf}\n\\caption{NVE ROC curve ($n{=}56$ configurations, AUROC${=}0.990$, comprehensive validation). The preset $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$ operating point (star) achieves TPR${=}1.00$, FPR${=}0.09$, accuracy${=}53/56$, F1${=}0.941$. Three false positives arise from non-CIVO value overestimation: mitigation transient VE spike (008e\\_v3 subsample, $1.93\\times$) and frozen embedding transients (047, $1.71\\times$; 057, $1.88\\times$). Threshold calibrated and validated post-hoc; cross-architecture validation deferred.}\n\\label{fig:nve_roc}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.7\\columnwidth]{../../artifacts/figures/nve_threshold_prf.pdf}\n\\caption{Precision, Recall, and F1 as a function of NVE threshold ($n{=}56$ configurations). Peak F1${=}0.941$ at $\\tau{=}1.5\\times$, where recall remains perfect ($1.00$) and precision${=}0.889$. The plateau of high F1 across $\\tau \\in [1.5, 1.7]$ indicates robustness to threshold selection.}\n\\label{fig:nve_prf}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe label a configuration as CIVO-positive if and only if (i)~it uses a trained $\\dt$-embedding with multi-rate data exposure, and (ii)~NVE exceeds $1.5\\times$ with co-occurring entropy collapse. Configurations exhibiting high NVE through other mechanisms (gradient truncation side effects, value anchor pollution) are labeled non-CIVO.\n\nThe three false positives (FPR${=}0.18$) are mechanistically distinct from CIVO: (1)~RAVE anchor pollution (exp\\_028, the EMA anchor becomes inflated, producing overestimation through a different pathway); (2)~obs-decoder SGS-induced value distortion (exp\\_025 seed~2, blocking observation decoder gradients damages the RSSM latent space); (3)~IRD eval-driven collapse (exp\\_008e\\_v3 seed~2, actor entropy \\emph{increases} with evaluation collapse, mechanistically distinct from CIVO's entropy \\emph{decrease}). All three produce NVE $> 1.5\\times$ through non-CIVO mechanisms, indicating that the NVE diagnostic detects value overestimation broadly rather than CIVO specifically---a feature rather than a limitation for practitioners monitoring deployment health. The closest sub-threshold case (008f seed~4, NVE${=}1.41\\times$) lacks $\\dt$-conditioning entirely ($d{=}0$); its elevated NVE reflects transient value noise that self-corrects without the positive feedback loop characteristic of CIVO. The closest above-threshold positive (008d seed~3, NVE${=}1.53\\times$) exhibits the full CIVO causal chain: trained $\\dt$-embedding, entropy decrease, and evaluation collapse to 14\\% of baseline.\n\n\\paragraph{Comprehensive validation ($n{=}56$).}\nExpanding the evaluation set to all classifiable experiments yields $n{=}56$ ($n_{\\text{pos}}{=}24$, $n_{\\text{neg}}{=}32$), spanning Walker Walk, Cheetah Run, and multiple intervention configurations (SGS, TCN, dual-critic, frozen embedding). At the $1.5\\times$ threshold: AUROC${=}0.990$, precision${=}0.889$, recall${=}1.000$, F1${=}0.941$, accuracy${=}53/56$. The three false positives are: exp\\_008e\\_v3\\_subsample ($1.93\\times$, mitigation config with transient VE spike), exp\\_047 ($1.71\\times$, frozen embedding transient), and exp\\_057 ($1.88\\times$, frozen embedding transient). All 24 CIVO-positive configurations are correctly detected (FN${=}0$), with CIVO-positive NVE ranging from $1.53\\times$ (008d seed~3, late-onset) to $70.64\\times$ (010b seed~0, Cheetah). The substantial increase in sample size from $n{=}32$ to $n{=}56$ strengthens confidence in the $1.5\\times$ threshold as a robust diagnostic boundary.\n\n\\subsection{NVE Normalization Robustness}\n\\label{sec:nve_robustness}","numLines":25,"startLine":253,"totalLines":1175}}}

Frozen final exceeds trained final; ordering is seed-dependent, ruling out $\\dt$-embedding causation.\\\\\n$^h$ PickCube environment transient: frozen control peak $8.81\\times$ at 62.5K, self-correcting to $0.55\\times$ at 72.5K; matches trained (exp\\_019b) trajectory.\\\\\n$^\\heartsuit$ Evaluated at 55K steps due to early termination of exp\\_008d; extended run (exp\\_009e) reached 131.08 at 105K.\\\\\n$^i$ Cartpole: exp\\_060 reports seed~2 eval only; exp\\_061/062 report 3-seed mean.\\\\\n$^j$ CIVO-unrelated learning stagnation; NVE $\\leq 0.6\\times$, no value overestimation.\\par}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-domain CIVO reproducibility. ``$\\dt$-emb-dep.'' indicates trained peaks consistently exceed frozen at matched timepoints across seeds.}\n\\label{tab:cross_domain}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Domain} & \\textbf{Environment} & \\textbf{Trained} & \\textbf{Frz ctrl} & \\textbf{$\\dt$-emb-dep.\\ CIVO} \\\\\n\\midrule\nLocomotion & Walker Walk & 5/5 & 0/3 & Yes (NVE $1.53$--$4.72\\times$) \\\\\nLocomotion & Cheetah Run & 2/2 & 0/2 & Yes (NVE $8.27$--$108.5\\times$) \\\\\nManipulation & StackCube-v1 & 0/2 & 0/1 & No (frozen$\\approx$trained) \\\\\nManipulation & PegInsertionSide & 0/2 & 0/2 & No (seed-dep.\\ ordering) \\\\\nManipulation & PickCube-v1 & 0/1 & 0/1 & No (frozen spike similar) \\\\\n\\midrule\nLocomotion & Cartpole Swingup & 0/3 & 0/3 & No (single-joint) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Cartpole Swingup.} Cartpole Swingup (single-DoF locomotion) serves as a cross-environment control. Three seeds of the CIVO-trigger configuration (exp\\_061: trained $e_{\\dt}$, subsample ON, $H{=}8$, $\\dt{=}2$) and three seeds of the no-embedding control (exp\\_062) both produce zero CIVO onset. Despite using the hyperparameter combination that triggers collapse in all 10 tested multi-joint locomotion seeds (Walker/Cheetah), the single-joint Cartpole shows no CIVO, confirming the phenomenon requires multi-joint dynamics where temporal abstraction amplifies the reward fidelity gap.\n\n\\section{NVE Threshold Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_sensitivity}\n\nWe report the full sensitivity analysis for the NVE diagnostic framework introduced in Section~\\ref{sec:results}. The main text reports Task~B2 (any CIVO detection, $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$). Here we additionally present Task~B1, which restricts the positive class to \\emph{sustained} CIVO, specifically experiments exhibiting persistent, accelerating value overestimation that progresses to performance collapse. Transient CIVO cases (e.g., exp~009b, which self-corrects after a brief excursion above the threshold) are classified as negative in B1.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Experiment NVE Values}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:nve_raw} lists the NVE values at 35K+ training steps for all eleven factorial experiments (including four 008e\\_v3 seeds and exp\\_059). Experiments are sorted by descending NVE. All four 008e\\_v3 seeds (mitigation configuration with $\\Delta t{=}1$ imagination) remain healthy with NVE $\\leq 0.6\\times$, confirming that restricting imagination to $\\Delta t{=}1$ effectively prevents CIVO.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-experiment NVE values (35K+ steps) and ground truth labels. $+$ = positive (CIVO), $-$ = negative. Trans.\\ = transient CIVO with self-correction.}\n\\label{tab:nve_raw}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nExperiment & NVE & Mechanism & B1 & B2 \\\\\n\\midrule\n006          & 4.83 & sustained CIVO & $+$ & $+$ \\\\\n059          & 4.72 & self-correcting CIVO & $-$ & $+$ \\\\\n008d         & 4.12 & sustained CIVO & $+$ & $+$ \\\\\n009b         & 1.62 & transient CIVO & $-$ & $+$ \\\\\n001          & 1.00 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\\\\n008f         & 0.97 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\\\\n008a         & 0.93 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\\\\n008e\\_v3\\_s0 & 0.60 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\\\\n008e\\_v3\\_s1 & 0.60 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\\\\n008e\\_v3\\_s5 & 0.60 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\\\\n008e\\_v3\\_s2 & 0.50 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Threshold Classification Performance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b1} reports Task~B1 classification metrics across six NVE thresholds. Task~B1 achieves $\\text{AUROC} = 0.944$ because exp\\_059 (NVE${=}4.72\\times$, self-correcting CIVO, B1-negative) ranks above the sustained-CIVO positive 008d ($4.12\\times$), breaking perfect rank separation. The optimal threshold lies in the interval $(1.62, 4.12]$; exp\\_059 is the sole false positive at $\\geq 1.7\\times$ (accuracy $10/11 = 90.9\\%$). Table~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2} provides the corresponding analysis for Task~B2 ($\\text{AUROC} = 1.000$, perfect separation with no false positives at $\\geq 1.2\\times$).\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{\\textbf{Task~B1}: Sustained CIVO detection sensitivity analysis ($n_{\\text{pos}}=2$, $n_{\\text{neg}}=9$, $n=11$, $\\text{AUROC}=0.944$). All confidence intervals are two-sided Clopper-Pearson exact binomial ($\\alpha=0.05$). $^\\dagger$Optimal threshold in $(1.62, 4.12]$; exp\\_059 (NVE${=}4.72\\times$, B1-negative) is the sole false positive.}\n\\label{tab:sensitivity_b1}\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nThreshold & TP & FP & FN & TN & Accuracy [95\\% CI] & Precision & Recall & F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\geq 1.0\\times$ & 2 & 3 & 0 & 6 & 72.7\\% \\; [39.0, 94.0] & 40.0\\% & 100\\% & 57.1\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 1.2\\times$ & 2 & 2 & 0 & 7 & 81.8\\% \\; [48.2, 97.7] & 50.0\\% & 100\\% & 66.7\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 1.5\\times$ & 2 & 2 & 0 & 7 & 81.8\\% \\; [48.2, 97.7] & 50.0\\% & 100\\% & 66.7\\% \\\\\n\\rowcolor{gray!15}\n$\\geq 1.7\\times^\\dagger$ & 2 & 1 & 0 & 8 & 90.9\\% \\; [58.7, 99.8] & 66.7\\% & 100\\% & 80.0\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 2.0\\times$ & 2 & 1 & 0 & 8 & 90.9\\% \\; [58.7, 99.8] & 66.7\\% & 100\\% & 80.0\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 3.9\\times$ & 2 & 1 & 0 & 8 & 90.9\\% \\; [58.7, 99.8] & 66.7\\% & 100\\% & 80.0\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{\\textbf{Task~B2}: Any CIVO detection sensitivity analysis ($n_{\\text{pos}}=4$, $n_{\\text{neg}}=7$, $n=11$, $\\text{AUROC}=1.000$). Perfect rank separation: all CIVO-positive experiments have NVE $> 1.5\\times$ and all negatives have NVE $\\leq 1.0\\times$. The $1.5\\times$ threshold (highlighted) achieves perfect classification.}\n\\label{tab:sensitivity_b2}\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nThreshold & TP & FP & FN & TN & Accuracy [95\\% CI] & Precision & Recall & F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\geq 1.0\\times$ & 4 & 1 & 0 & 6 & 90.9\\% \\; [58.7, 99.8] & 80.0\\% & 100\\% & 88.9\\% \\\\\n\\rowcolor{gray!15}\n$\\geq 1.2\\times$ & 4 & 0 & 0 & 7 & 100.0\\% \\; [71.5, 100.0] & 100\\% & 100\\% & 100\\% \\\\\n\\rowcolor{gray!15}\n$\\geq 1.5\\times$ & 4 & 0 & 0 & 7 & 100.0\\% \\; [71.5, 100.0] & 100\\% & 100\\% & 100\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 1.7\\times$ & 3 & 0 & 1 & 7 & 90.9\\% \\; [58.7, 99.8] & 100\\% & 75.0\\% & 85.7\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 2.0\\times$ & 3 & 0 & 1 & 7 & 90.9\\% \\; [58.7, 99.8] & 100\\% & 75.0\\% & 85.7\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 3.9\\times$ & 3 & 0 & 1 & 7 & 90.9\\% \\; [58.7, 99.8] & 100\\% & 75.0\\% & 85.7\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{\\textbf{Task~B2 (full evaluation set)}: Any CIVO detection across two environments ($n_{\\text{pos}}=8$, $n_{\\text{neg}}=9$, $n=17$, $\\text{AUROC}=1.000$). Includes Walker Walk (15 configurations) and Cheetah Run (2 configurations). Perfect rank separation; all CIVO-positive NVE $\\geq 1.53\\times$, all negatives $\\leq 1.04\\times$.}\n\\label{tab:sensitivity_b2_full}\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nThreshold & TP & FP & FN & TN & Accuracy [95\\% CI] & Precision & Recall & F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\geq 1.0\\times$ & 8 & 2 & 0 & 7 & 88.2\\% \\; [63.6, 98.5] & 80.0\\% & 100\\% & 88.9\\% \\\\\n\\rowcolor{gray!15}\n$\\geq 1.2\\times$ & 8 & 0 & 0 & 9 & 100.0\\% \\; [80.5, 100.0] & 100\\% & 100\\% & 100\\% \\\\\n\\rowcolor{gray!15}\n$\\geq 1.5\\times$ & 8 & 0 & 0 & 9 & 100.0\\% \\; [80.5, 100.0] & 100\\% & 100\\% & 100\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 1.7\\times$ & 7 & 0 & 1 & 9 & 94.1\\% \\; [71.3, 99.9] & 100\\% & 87.5\\% & 93.3\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 2.0\\times$ & 7 & 0 & 1 & 9 & 94.1\\% \\; [71.3, 99.9] & 100\\% & 87.5\\% & 93.3\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 3.9\\times$ & 6 & 0 & 2 & 9 & 88.2\\% \\; [63.6, 98.5] & 100\\% & 75.0\\% & 85.7\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\subsection{Threshold Recommendation}\n\nFor the main diagnostic (Task~B2), we recommend $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$ as the operating threshold for three reasons:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{Perfect classification.} The $1.5\\times$ threshold achieves 100\\% accuracy on the $n{=}11$ factorial set: all four CIVO cases (006, 059, 008d, 009b) are detected, and no non-CIVO experiment exceeds the threshold (AUROC${=}1.000$, F1${=}100\\%$).\n\\item \\textbf{Maximum recall with margin.} The lowest CIVO-positive NVE ($1.62\\times$, 009b) clears the threshold by $0.12\\times$; the highest negative ($1.00\\times$, 001) sits $0.50\\times$ below it.\n\\item \\textbf{Robustness.} The $1.5\\times$ threshold achieves perfect classification across the extended $n{=}17$ two-environment evaluation (Table~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2_full}), with the nearest positive--negative gap widening further ($1.53\\times$ vs.\\ $1.04\\times$).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nFor Task~B1 (sustained CIVO only), the optimal threshold $\\nve \\geq 1.7\\times$ achieves $10/11$ accuracy ($90.9\\%$, 95\\% CI: [58.7\\%, 99.8\\%]); the sole false positive is exp\\_059 (NVE${=}4.72\\times$, self-correcting CIVO classified as B1-negative).\n\n\\subsection{Expanded ROC Analysis}\n\\label{sec:roc_analysis}\n\nThe original $n{=}17$ analysis (Tables~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b1}--\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2_full}) predates the SGS, RAVE, and TCN experiments. We extend the NVE diagnostic evaluation to $n{=}56$ configurations spanning all tested interventions. Figure~\\ref{fig:nve_roc} shows the receiver operating characteristic curve with 95\\% bootstrap confidence interval (10{,}000 resamples).\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.7\\columnwidth]{../../artifacts/figures/nve_roc_curve.pdf}\n\\caption{NVE ROC curve ($n{=}56$ configurations, AUROC${=}0.990$, comprehensive validation). The preset $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$ operating point (star) achieves TPR${=}1.00$, FPR${=}0.09$, accuracy${=}53/56$, F1${=}0.941$. Three false positives arise from non-CIVO value overestimation: mitigation transient VE spike (008e\\_v3 subsample, $1.93\\times$) and frozen embedding transients (047, $1.71\\times$; 057, $1.88\\times$). Threshold calibrated and validated post-hoc; cross-architecture validation deferred.}\n\\label{fig:nve_roc}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.7\\columnwidth]{../../artifacts/figures/nve_threshold_prf.pdf}\n\\caption{Precision, Recall, and F1 as a function of NVE threshold ($n{=}56$ configurations). Peak F1${=}0.941$ at $\\tau{=}1.5\\times$, where recall remains perfect ($1.00$) and precision${=}0.889$. The plateau of high F1 across $\\tau \\in [1.5, 1.7]$ indicates robustness to threshold selection.}\n\\label{fig:nve_prf}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe label a configuration as CIVO-positive if and only if (i)~it uses a trained $\\dt$-embedding with multi-rate data exposure, and (ii)~NVE exceeds $1.5\\times$ with co-occurring entropy collapse. Configurations exhibiting high NVE through other mechanisms (gradient truncation side effects, value anchor pollution) are labeled non-CIVO.\n\nThe three false positives (FPR${=}0.18$) are mechanistically distinct from CIVO: (1)~RAVE anchor pollution (exp\\_028, the EMA anchor becomes inflated, producing overestimation through a different pathway); (2)~obs-decoder SGS-induced value distortion (exp\\_025 seed~2, blocking observation decoder gradients damages the RSSM latent space); (3)~IRD eval-driven collapse (exp\\_008e\\_v3 seed~2, actor entropy \\emph{increases} with evaluation collapse, mechanistically distinct from CIVO's entropy \\emph{decrease}). All three produce NVE $> 1.5\\times$ through non-CIVO mechanisms, indicating that the NVE diagnostic detects value overestimation broadly rather than CIVO specifically---a feature rather than a limitation for practitioners monitoring deployment health. The closest sub-threshold case (008f seed~4, NVE${=}1.41\\times$) lacks $\\dt$-conditioning entirely ($d{=}0$); its elevated NVE reflects transient value noise that self-corrects without the positive feedback loop characteristic of CIVO. The closest above-threshold positive (008d seed~3, NVE${=}1.53\\times$) exhibits the full CIVO causal chain: trained $\\dt$-embedding, entropy decrease, and evaluation collapse to 14\\% of baseline.\n\n\\paragraph{Comprehensive validation ($n{=}56$).}\nExpanding the evaluation set to all classifiable experiments yields $n{=}56$ ($n_{\\text{pos}}{=}24$, $n_{\\text{neg}}{=}32$), spanning Walker Walk, Cheetah Run, and multiple intervention configurations (SGS, TCN, dual-critic, frozen embedding). At the $1.5\\times$ threshold: AUROC${=}0.990$, precision${=}0.889$, recall${=}1.000$, F1${=}0.941$, accuracy${=}53/56$. The three false positives are: exp\\_008e\\_v3\\_subsample ($1.93\\times$, mitigation config with transient VE spike), exp\\_047 ($1.71\\times$, frozen embedding transient), and exp\\_057 ($1.88\\times$, frozen embedding transient). All 24 CIVO-positive configurations are correctly detected (FN${=}0$), with CIVO-positive NVE ranging from $1.53\\times$ (008d seed~3, late-onset) to $70.64\\times$ (010b seed~0, Cheetah). The substantial increase in sample size from $n{=}32$ to $n{=}56$ strengthens confidence in the $1.5\\times$ threshold as a robust diagnostic boundary.\n\n\\subsection{NVE Normalization Robustness}\n\\label{sec:nve_robustness}\n\nTo assess sensitivity to baseline seed choice, we extend the single-seed baseline (seed~1, eval 915.7 at 105K) to three additional seeds trained to 505K steps (Table~\\ref{tab:baseline_seeds}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Walker Walk baseline seeds at 105K and 505K steps.}\n\\label{tab:baseline_seeds}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Seed} & \\textbf{Eval@105K} & \\textbf{Eval@505K} & \\textbf{ve@65K} & \\textbf{ve@105K} \\\\\n\\midrule\ns1 & 915.7 & 970.4 & 0.563 & 0.656 \\\\\ns2 & 443.5 & 945.7 & 0.898 & 0.742 \\\\\ns3 & 869.0 & 947.7 & --- & 0.664 \\\\\ns4 & 922.8 & 938.9 & --- & 0.624 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nSeed~2 exhibits slow convergence (eval 443.5 at 105K, recovering to 945.7 by 512K), confirming non-pathological slow convergence rather than seed failure. Its elevated $v/e$ ratio at 65K (0.898 vs.\\ 0.563 for seed~1) reflects the low evaluation denominator rather than pathological value overestimation. Using seed~2 as the sole normalization reference systematically deflates NVE values, causing two marginal CIVO-positive configurations (008d seed~3, peak NVE $1.53\\times$; 009b seed~2, peak NVE $1.62\\times$) to fall below the $1.5\\times$ threshold. Core CIVO cases (peak NVE $> 3\\times$) remain correctly classified under all baseline seeds.\n\nSingle-seed normalization (s1) yields AUROC $= 0.933$ and accuracy 7/8 (87.5\\%) on the Walker subset. Four-seed mean normalization yields AUROC $= 0.867$ and accuracy 6/8 (75.0\\%): the boundary case exp\\_009b flips from $\\nve = 1.62\\times$ (s1) to $1.23\\times$ (4-seed mean), because its peak occurs at 45K when the s1 $v/e$ ratio (0.331) is well below the 4-seed mean (0.436). All high-NVE configurations remain correctly classified (006: $4.84\\times \\to 4.52\\times$; 008d: $4.12\\times \\to 3.54\\times$). The $1.5\\times$ threshold thus correctly identifies all strong CIVO cases regardless of baseline choice; sensitivity is confined to configurations within 10\\% of the threshold.\n\n\\paragraph{Normalization conventions.} Walker NVE is normalized against the single-seed baseline (seed~1, exp\\_001, 505K steps). Cheetah NVE is normalized against a 2-seed mean baseline (exp\\_077 seed~0, eval 448.1; exp\\_083 seed~1, eval 444.0; mean 446.1 at 105K). Both provide sufficient coverage of the respective experimental windows (Walker: 210K analysis window; Cheetah: 105K analysis window).\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.} The initial $n{=}11$ analysis had wide Clopper-Pearson confidence intervals (e.g., 90.9\\% accuracy, 95\\% CI [58.7\\%, 99.8\\%]). The comprehensive evaluation ($n{=}56$, AUROC${=}0.990$, F1${=}0.941$; Figure~\\ref{fig:nve_roc}) substantially tightens these bounds. Table~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2_full} reports the $n{=}17$ two-environment core evaluation. Cross-architecture validation is deferred to future work.\n\n\\subsection{Leave-One-Environment-Out Cross-Validation}\n\\label{sec:nve_loeo}\n\nTo assess whether $\\theta{=}1.5\\times$ generalizes beyond the in-sample evaluation, we perform leave-one-environment-out (LOEO) cross-validation across Walker ($n{=}15$), Cheetah ($n{=}5$), and PickCube ($n{=}2$). For each fold, we hold out all experiments from one environment, select the optimal accuracy-maximizing threshold range on the remaining training set, and evaluate the fixed $\\theta{=}1.5\\times$ on the held-out environment.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Leave-one-environment-out cross-validation of NVE threshold $\\theta=1.5\\times$.\nFor each fold, the threshold is validated on an environment whose data was \\emph{not} used\nto select $\\theta$. The optimal threshold range on each training set is reported;\n$\\theta=1.5$ falls within the optimal range in all three folds.}\n\\label{tab:nve_loeo}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHeld-out & $N_{\\text{train}}$ & $N_{\\text{test}}$ & $\\theta_{\\text{opt}}$ range & Test Accuracy & Test AUROC \\\\\n\\midrule\nPickCube & 20 & 2 & $(1.45,\\, 1.53]$ & 2/2 (1.000) & 1.000 \\\\\nCheetah  & 17 & 5 & $(1.45,\\, 1.53]$ & 5/5 (1.000) & 1.000 \\\\\nWalker   &  7 & 15 & $(1.00,\\, 3.78)$ & 14/15 (0.933) & 0.963 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{Mean} & — & — & — & \\textbf{0.978} & \\textbf{0.988} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe threshold $\\theta{=}1.5\\times$ falls within the optimal accuracy range for all three training folds: $(1.45, 1.53]$ when Walker data is available, and the wider $(1.00, 3.78)$ when training on Cheetah$+$PickCube only (where CIVO and non-CIVO NVE values are well-separated). The single cross-validation error is exp\\_008e\\_v3 seed~2 (Walker), an IRD collapse mechanistically distinct from CIVO (\\S\\ref{sec:roc_analysis}). Mean held-out accuracy is 0.978 and mean AUROC is 0.988, confirming that $\\theta{=}1.5\\times$ generalizes across environments without retuning.\nPickCube fold confidence intervals remain wide (Clopper-Pearson 95\\% CI: $[0.158, 1.000]$) due to $N_{\\text{test}}{=}2$.\n\n\\subsection{Leave-One-Config-Out Cross-Validation}\n\\label{sec:nve_loco}\n\n\\paragraph{Leave-One-Config-Out Cross-Validation.}\nTo rule out threshold overfitting to any particular experimental configuration, we perform leave-one-config-out (LOCO) cross-validation over $K{=}12$ configuration groups (e.g., \\texttt{008d\\_civo\\_trigger}, \\texttt{frozen\\_dt\\_emb}, \\texttt{tcn}; $n{=}56$ total). Each fold holds out all experiments from one configuration, selects the F1-maximizing threshold on the remaining $K{-}1$ groups, and evaluates on the held-out group.\n\nIn $10/12$ folds, the training-optimal threshold falls within $\\pm 0.05$ of $\\theta{=}1.5\\times$ (mean $1.61 \\pm 0.23$, range $[1.47, 2.12]$). The two folds selecting a higher threshold ($\\theta{=}2.1$; \\texttt{008d\\_civo\\_trigger} and \\texttt{tcn}, both predominantly CIVO-positive) perform \\emph{worse} on their held-out sets than $\\theta{=}1.5$ (accuracy $0.78$/$0.88$ vs.\\ $1.00$/$1.00$), confirming that the fixed threshold generalizes better than per-fold optimization in these cases. Cross-validated accuracy at $\\theta{=}1.5$ is $53/56 = 94.6\\%$, identical to the in-sample result, with train AUROC consistently high across folds ($0.988 \\pm 0.003$). The three misclassifications are the same mechanistically distinct false positives identified in \\S\\ref{sec:roc_analysis}: two frozen-embedding transients (exp\\_047, exp\\_057) and one mitigation-config transient (exp\\_008e\\_v3\\_subsample). No CIVO-positive configuration is missed in any fold (FN${=}0$ across all 12 folds).\n\n% --- APPENDIX B: Factorial Ablation Details ---\n\n\\section{Factorial Ablation Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_factorial}\n\nThis appendix provides the full factorial ablation results summarized in \\S\\ref{sec:f3f5_summary} of the main text.\n\n\\paragraph{Embedding gradient analysis.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:three_way_emb} isolates embedding \\emph{trainability} as the necessary condition for CIVO by comparing four gradient conditions under otherwise identical configurations ($H{=}8$, subsample ON, $\\dt{=}2$ imagination).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{$\\dt$-embedding gradient conditions on Walker Walk. Any gradient pathway updating $e_{\\dt}$ is sufficient to induce CIVO; freezing or removing the embedding dramatically reduces severity.}\n\\label{tab:three_way_emb}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccl}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Grad flow & Exp(s) & NVE peak & Eval & CIVO \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained & all & 008d, 059 & $1.53$--$4.72\\times$ & 64--268 & 5/5 \\\\\nReward detach & obs+KL only & 044 & $4.45\\times$ & 44$\\to$538$^\\dagger$ & 1/1 \\\\\nFull SGS & KL only & 018 & $1.66\\times$ & 448 & 1/1$^*$ \\\\\nFrozen & none & 042, 015b, 047, 057 & $0.19$--$1.88\\times$$^\\ddagger$ & 204--612 & 2/4 \\\\\nRemoved & N/A & 008f & $0.97$--$1.41\\times$ & 307--503 & 0/3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{1pt}\n{\\raggedright\\footnotesize $^*$Full-SGS seed~1 (NVE $1.66\\times$); seed~2 collapsed. $^\\dagger$Self-corrected at ${\\sim}175$K (eval 44$\\to$538, 59\\% baseline; 224K run). $^\\ddagger$Exp\\_047 (NVE $1.71\\times$) and Exp\\_057 (NVE $1.88\\times$): both transient, self-correcting within 20K steps.\\par}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Controlled three-way causal ablation.}\nExperiments 067--069 provide a minimal causal ablation on Walker Walk (seed${=}1$), varying \\emph{only} the $\\dt$-embedding treatment while holding all other hyperparameters fixed ($H{=}8$, subsample ON, $\\dt{=}2$ imagination). Table~\\ref{tab:causal_ablation} shows that trained $\\dt$-embedding is necessary and sufficient for CIVO: freezing the embedding (gradient flow blocked, embedding weights still present) eliminates CIVO as completely as removing the embedding ($d{=}0$), ruling out the alternative hypothesis that embedding \\emph{presence} alone causes CIVO. Combined with all prior seeds, trained $\\dt$-embedding triggers CIVO in 10/10 multi-joint locomotion seeds (8 Walker, 2 Cheetah) versus 0/8 controls (Fisher exact $p < 0.0001$).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Three-way causal ablation (Walker Walk, seed${=}1$). Only the $\\dt$-embedding treatment varies; all other configuration is identical. Trained embedding triggers CIVO; frozen and removed do not.}\n\\label{tab:causal_ablation}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{llccl}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Exp} & \\textbf{Embedding} & \\textbf{NVE peak} & \\textbf{Eval final} & \\textbf{CIVO} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\rowcolor{gray!15}\n068 & Trained ($d{=}32$) & $6.57\\times$ & 39.1 & Yes (alert @85K) \\\\\n067 & Frozen ($d{=}32$, \\texttt{req\\_grad=F}) & $0.40\\times$ & 281.5 & No \\\\\n069 & Removed ($d{=}0$) & $0.66\\times$ & 237.8 & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe reward-detach condition (exp\\_044, \\texttt{reward\\_dt\\_emb\\_detach=True}) uses a backward hook to block reward-head gradients from reaching $e_{\\dt}$, complementing the framework-level Reward SGS (exp\\_016e). Both yield the same directional result---CIVO onset not prevented---ruling out implementation artifacts. Exp\\_044 exhibits delayed CIVO onset (${\\sim}95$K vs.\\ typical $25$--$35$K), consistent with reduced but non-zero gradient pressure on $e_{\\dt}$. Over a full 224K run, exp\\_044 self-corrects: eval recovers from 44 (95K) to 538 (215K, 59\\% baseline), but this recovery is incomplete compared to spontaneous recovery without gradient intervention (exp\\_045: 868, 95\\% baseline). The lower asymptote suggests that selectively disrupting gradient flow may create optimization difficulties beyond CIVO itself.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:factorial_design} summarizes the experimental configurations.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Factorial design. Each row specifies one experimental configuration. \\textbf{Emb}: $\\dt$-embedding mode. \\textbf{Sub}: temporal subsampling. \\textbf{H}: imagination horizon. \\textbf{Imag $\\dt$}: temporal resolution used during imagination.}\n\\label{tab:factorial_design}\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{ID} & \\textbf{Purpose} & \\textbf{Emb} & \\textbf{Sub} & \\textbf{H} & \\textbf{Imag $\\dt$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n001 & Reference baseline & --- & --- & 15 & 1 \\\\\n006 & Fixed $\\dt{=}2$ & trn & on & 15 & 2 \\\\\n007 & Fixed $\\dt{=}4$ & trn & on & 15 & 4 \\\\\n\\midrule\n008a & Unexposed emb ctrl & exp$^a$ & off & 15 & 2 \\\\\n008c & Horizon control & exp$^a$ & off & 8 & 2 \\\\\n008d & Collapse condition & trn & on & 8 & 2 \\\\\n008e\\_v3 & $\\dt{=}1$ imag control & trn & on & 15 & 1 \\\\\n008f & Causal ablation & --- & on & 8 & 2 \\\\\n\\midrule\n015b & Frozen-emb ctrl & frz$^b$ & on & 8 & 2 \\\\\n016e & Stop-grad (reward) & trn$^\\dagger$ & on & 8 & 2 \\\\\n018 & Full-SGS & trn$^\\ddagger$ & on & 8 & 2 \\\\\n\\midrule\n005 & Learned gate & adaptive & off & 15 & learned \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{-2pt}\n{\\raggedright\\footnotesize\ntrn = trained ($d{=}32$, exposed to $\\dt{>}1$ data via subsample ON);\nexp$^a$ = trained ($d{=}32$) but never exposed to $\\dt{>}1$ data (subsample OFF);\nfrz$^b$ = frozen random projection ($d{=}32$, \\texttt{requires\\_grad=False});\n--- = removed ($d{=}0$).\\\\\n$^\\dagger$ \\texttt{reward\\_dt\\_emb\\_detach=True}; $^\\ddagger$ all non-KL gradients to \\texttt{dt\\_emb} detached.\\par}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{F3: Unexposed Temporal Embedding Suppresses Value Drift}\n\\label{sec:f3}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig3_factorial_grid.pdf}\n\\caption{Factorial isolation of CIVO trigger. Only the combination of trained $e_{\\dt}$ and multi-rate data (upper-left cell) produces pathological NVE. Neither factor alone is sufficient.}\n\\label{fig:factorial_grid}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe empirical finding that trained embedding \\emph{plus} multi-rate exposure jointly predict CIVO rests on two complementary controls that each remove one factor while holding the other constant.\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence A: exposure is necessary (exp\\_008a).}\nExp\\_008a uses a trained embedding ($d{=}32$, gradients enabled) that is never exposed to $\\dt{>}1$ transitions during world-model training (subsample OFF). Despite sharing the same embedding architecture as exp\\_008d, its NVE remains in the $0.83$--$0.93\\times$ range across 3 seeds (105K steps). Performance follows two V-shaped recovery cycles, reaching 777.7 at 105K (85\\% of baseline). The critical factor is not the embedding's capacity to represent temporal information, but its adaptation to multi-rate data that enables precise but inaccurate $\\dt{>}1$ state predictions within the RSSM.\n\nThis contrasts with exp\\_006 (trained $e_{\\dt}$, subsample ON, $H{=}15$), where NVE accelerates from $1.61\\times$ at 45K to $2.31\\times$ at 55K before catastrophic collapse to 132.9 at 65K. Both experiments use identical trained embeddings ($d{=}32$, gradients enabled) and $H{=}15$, differing only in whether the world model sees $\\dt{>}1$ transitions during training. This confirms that multi-rate data exposure, not embedding architecture, is the operative variable.\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence B: trainability is necessary (exp\\_015b).}\nExp\\_015b uses a frozen random-projection embedding ($d{=}32$, \\texttt{requires\\_grad=False}, subsample ON, $H{=}8$), the same multi-rate data regime as exp\\_008d, but with a non-trainable temporal signal. Despite full exposure to $\\dt{>}1$ transitions, the NVE peaks at $1.45\\times$ at 55K but does not sustain escalation: it retreats to $0.65\\times$ by 65K and settles at $0.42\\times$ by 105K (trajectory: $0.81 \\rightarrow 1.26 \\rightarrow 1.45 \\rightarrow 0.65 \\rightarrow 1.05 \\rightarrow 0.47 \\rightarrow 0.39 \\rightarrow 0.42$). The transient peak approaches but does not exceed the $1.5\\times$ CIVO threshold, and critically, the ratio self-corrects rather than exhibiting the monotonic escalation seen in trained-embedding runs. Final evaluation reaches only 204.2 at 105K, low but through a qualitatively different failure mode than CIVO. Actor loss approaches zero (gradient starvation; see \\S\\ref{sec:pathways}), the frozen embedding provides no useful gradient signal for temporal adaptation.\n\nThe contrast with exp\\_008d is stark: under identical data conditions (subsample ON, $H{=}8$), the trained embedding drives NVE to $4.12\\times$ with sustained monotonic escalation ($1.78 \\rightarrow 1.71 \\rightarrow 2.09 \\rightarrow 4.12$) and precedes full collapse (eval 82.8), while the frozen embedding peaks transiently at $1.45\\times$ before self-correcting to $0.42\\times$, a qualitative difference between sustained and transient overestimation. This demonstrates that a $\\dt$-embedding must be able to \\emph{co-adapt} with RSSM dynamics to produce the systematic reward-prediction bias that drives CIVO; a static random projection, regardless of its exposure to multi-rate data, cannot create this bias.\n\nTogether, these two controls establish that in all tested configurations, CIVO occurs only with the \\emph{joint} presence of (i)~a trainable embedding that can co-adapt with RSSM dynamics, and (ii)~multi-rate training data that drives this co-adaptation toward biased temporal predictions. Neither factor alone is sufficient.\n\nCross-environment validation on Cheetah Run replicates the empirical pattern: trained $e_{\\dt}$ precedes CIVO in both tested seeds (exp\\_010b, 2 seeds, NVE $8.27$--$108.52\\times$, collapse to 51.7/0.83) while removing the embedding eliminates it (exp\\_024b, 2 seeds, NVE $<0.2\\times$, eval 96.5/77.3). Freezing the embedding on Cheetah (exp\\_046, NVE${=}1.35\\times$ vs.\\ trained $70.64\\times$, a $52\\times$ reduction) further confirms that embedding trainability, not architecture, drives CIVO. This extends the sufficiency finding from Walker Walk to a second locomotion environment with qualitatively different dynamics (6-joint vs.\\ 2-joint).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Causal isolation: $e_{\\dt}$ configuration $\\times$ multi-rate data exposure. The combination of trained embedding and multi-rate exposure is the sole predictor of CIVO in tested configurations. Neither factor alone is sufficient.}\n\\label{tab:causal_matrix}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n& Multi-rate data (\\textsc{on}) & Single-rate data (\\textsc{off}) \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained $e_{\\dt}$ & exp\\_008d: \\textbf{CIVO} & exp\\_008a: healthy \\\\\n & NVE 4.12$\\times$, collapse & NVE 0.93$\\times$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nFrozen $e_{\\dt}$ & exp\\_015b: grad.\\ starvation & --- \\\\\n & peak NVE 1.45$\\times$ (transient) & \\\\\n\\midrule\nRemoved $e_{\\dt}$ & exp\\_008f: no CIVO & --- \\\\\n & NVE $\\leq$0.97$\\times$ & \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOnly the upper-left cell (trained embedding with multi-rate exposure) precedes CIVO (Table~\\ref{tab:causal_matrix}). This 2$\\times$2 structure establishes that both factors are jointly sufficient in tested configurations.\n\n\\subsection{F4: Degradation Scales Monotonically with \\texorpdfstring{$\\dt$}{dt}}\n\\label{sec:f4}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig5_dt_amplification.pdf}\n\\caption{Peak evaluation return as a function of imagination $\\dt$. Performance degrades monotonically: $\\dt{=}1$ (915.7) $>$ $\\dt{=}2$ (485.3, $-47\\%$) $>$ $\\dt{=}4$ (176.9, $-81\\%$).}\n\\label{fig:dt_amplification}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe magnitude of degradation scales monotonically with the imagination step size (Figure~\\ref{fig:dt_amplification}). From the $\\dt{=}1$ baseline (915.7 at 105K), fixed $\\dt{=}2$ (exp\\_006) achieves a peak of only 485.3 ($-47\\%$), and fixed $\\dt{=}4$ (exp\\_007) peaks at 176.9 ($-81\\%$). This monotonic relationship is consistent with compounding model error: larger temporal gaps amplify the RSSM's prediction inaccuracy at each imagination step, and these errors accumulate across the rollout horizon.\n\n\\subsection{F5: Imagination Horizon and Subsampling Are Secondary Factors}\n\\label{sec:f5}\n\nNeither horizon length nor temporal subsampling precedes collapse without multi-rate data exposure: both trained-but-unexposed configurations (008a, 008c) maintain $\\nve < 0.93\\times$ (\\S\\ref{sec:f3}), while the trained-and-exposed counterpart (008d) collapses with $\\nve = 4.12\\times$.  The no-embedding configurations show cross-seed variance (008f: 503.0 vs.\\ 009c: 148.3) but neither exhibits $\\nve > 1.5\\times$; low evaluation alone does not indicate CIVO.\n\nThe $3.2\\times$ performance gap (008a: 777.7 vs.\\ 008c: 240.9) quantifies the horizon effect independently of CIVO, as both conditions maintain healthy NVE ($\\leq$0.93$\\times$).\n\n% --- APPENDIX C: Full Analysis & Discussion ---\n\n\\section{Full Analysis \\& Discussion}\n\\label{sec:appendix_analysis}\n\nThis appendix provides the complete mechanistic analysis summarized in Section~\\ref{sec:discussion} of the main text.\n\n\\subsection{The CIVO Mechanism}\n\\label{sec:civo_mechanism}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig4_mechanism_diagram.pdf}\n\\caption{CIVO mechanism diagram. The trained $\\dt$-embedding enables asymmetric reward prediction fidelity, initiating a feedback loop: biased RSSM rewards $\\to$ optimistic critic values $\\to$ actor exploitation $\\to$ reinforced state-distribution skew.}\n\\label{fig:civo_mechanism}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{RSSM fidelity at $\\dt{=}1$ vs.\\ $\\dt{=}2$. Trained embedding (008d) produces $2.78\\times$ reward gap; removing it (008f) eliminates the gap but degrades absolute prediction.}\n\\label{tab:rssm_fidelity}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{obs\\_MSE}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{reward\\_MAE}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\n\\textbf{Config} & $\\dt{=}1$ & $\\dt{=}2$ & Gap & $\\dt{=}1$ & $\\dt{=}2$ & Gap \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline & 13.49 & 27.50 & 2.04$\\times$ & 0.0102 & 0.0159 & 1.56$\\times$ \\\\\n008d (trained) & 41.31 & 53.67 & 1.30$\\times$ & 0.0568 & 0.1579 & 2.78$\\times$ \\\\\n008f (no emb) & 8.01 & 22.04 & 2.75$\\times$ & 0.7469 & 0.7526 & 1.01$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe RSSM fidelity analysis (Table~\\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}) reveals the mechanistic core of CIVO (Figure~\\ref{fig:civo_mechanism}).  The trained $\\dt$-embedding enables the reward head to learn temporally differentiated predictions, but these predictions are asymmetrically accurate: reward MAE at $\\dt{=}1$ is $0.0568$, while at $\\dt{=}2$ it rises to $0.1579$, a $2.78\\times$ gap.  This gap is not an artifact of prediction difficulty alone; the no-embedding configuration (008f) exhibits a $1.01\\times$ gap, and the baseline shows $1.56\\times$.  The trained embedding actively amplifies the fidelity discrepancy by allowing the reward head to co-adapt with the temporal signal.\n\nThis asymmetric fidelity initiates a feedback loop with four stages: (1) the trained $\\dt$-embedding causes RSSM predictions at $\\dt > 1$ to carry systematically biased reward estimates; (2) the critic, trained entirely on imagined trajectories, internalizes these biased rewards as optimistic value estimates; (3) the actor exploits the overestimated regions of the value landscape, shifting its policy toward states that appear high-value only under the biased model; and (4) this policy shift further skews the distribution of imagined states, reinforcing the bias.  The diagnostic signature is a sustained $\\nve$ above $1.5\\times$, followed by evaluation collapse.\n\nA random-policy control experiment ($N{=}100$ rollouts, 95\\% bootstrap CI) decomposes CIVO into two necessary stages.  \\textbf{Stage~1 (dynamics bias):} even under a random policy, $\\dt{=}2$ imagination produces $1.68\\times$ higher mean reward than $\\dt{=}1$ in the trained-embedding model (008d): the RSSM prior already generates reward-favorable trajectories at $\\dt > 1$, independent of actor behavior.  \\textbf{Stage~2 (actor exploitation):} the trained actor learns to exploit these biased regions, amplifying the ratio to $2.90\\times$.  In the no-embedding control (008f), neither stage is present: the random-policy ratio is $1.11\\times$ and the trained-actor ratio is $0.98\\times$.  The decomposition attributes $\\approx 36\\%$ of the total effect to dynamics bias and $\\approx 64\\%$ to actor exploitation, which confirms that both stages are necessary for CIVO to manifest.\n\nThis mechanism is analogous to delusional bias \\citep{lu2018nondelusional}, but differs in origin: delusional bias arises from policy-class constraints, while CIVO is model-induced.  The consequence (policy exploitation of overestimated regions) is similar, but the causal pathway is distinct.\n\n\\paragraph{Signed reward error analysis.}\nTo test whether CIVO arises from one-step reward overprediction, we compute signed error (predicted $-$ actual) on posterior-conditioned RSSM predictions (Table~\\ref{tab:signed_error}). The trained-embedding model (008d) shows \\emph{negative} bias at $\\dt{=}2$ (mean signed error $-0.599$, only 8\\% of samples overpredicted), ruling out observation-level reward overprediction as the mechanism. The baseline is well-calibrated (mean SE $\\approx 0$), confirming methodological soundness. Value overestimation therefore occurs during \\emph{imagination-level} prior drift: the RSSM prior (unconditioned on observations) generates reward-favorable trajectories at $\\dt > 1$ (Stage~1 above, $1.68\\times$ ratio under random policy), and the critic accumulates these into overestimated returns. The one-step reward prediction is negatively biased when conditioned on real observations, but this posterior correction is unavailable during imagination. This also distinguishes CIVO from generic compounding error, which would produce \\emph{positive} signed error accumulating with horizon; the negative observation-level bias confirms that CIVO is a prior-drift phenomenon, not a reward-prediction artifact.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Signed reward error (predicted $-$ actual) on posterior-conditioned RSSM predictions. Negative values indicate underprediction. The trained-embedding model underpredicts at $\\dt{=}2$, ruling out observation-level reward overprediction as the CIVO mechanism.}\n\\label{tab:signed_error}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Config} & $\\dt$ & \\textbf{Mean SE} & \\textbf{Std} & \\textbf{\\%Over} & \\textbf{Actual} & \\textbf{Pred} \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline & 1 & $+0.001$ & 0.023 & 39.6\\% & 1.893 & 1.894 \\\\\n\\midrule\n008d (trained) & 1 & $+0.018$ & 0.072 & 61.8\\% & 0.361 & 0.379 \\\\\n008d (trained) & 2 & $\\mathbf{-0.599}$ & 0.722 & 8.0\\% & 0.721 & 0.121 \\\\\n\\midrule\n008f (no emb) & 1 & $-0.710$ & 0.704 & 14.0\\% & 0.921 & 0.211 \\\\\n008f (no emb) & 2 & $-1.645$ & 1.485 & 5.0\\% & 1.840 & 0.195 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Embedding geometry (exp\\_065).}\nTo characterize what the trained $\\dt$-embedding learns, we measure pairwise cosine similarity between $e_{\\dt{=}1}$ and $e_{\\dt{=}2}$ across training checkpoints for three trained seeds (two environments) and two frozen controls (Table~\\ref{tab:cosine_sim}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cosine similarity between $e_{\\dt{=}1}$ and $e_{\\dt{=}2}$ vectors. Trained embeddings converge to near-opposite representations; frozen embeddings remain near-orthogonal throughout.}\n\\label{tab:cosine_sim}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Config} & \\textbf{Env} & \\textbf{cos@25K} & \\textbf{cos@75K} & \\textbf{Status} \\\\\n\\midrule\n008d s3 (trained) & Walker & $-0.87$ & $-0.70$ & CIVO \\\\\n059 s5 (trained) & Walker & $-0.94$ & $-0.86$ & CIVO \\\\\n010b s0 (trained) & Cheetah & $-0.90$ & $-0.84$ & CIVO \\\\\n\\midrule\n057 s2 (frozen) & Walker & $-0.15$ & $-0.15$ & Healthy \\\\\n058 s1 (frozen) & Cheetah & $+0.07$ & $+0.07$ & Healthy \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll trained seeds map $\\dt{=}1$ and $\\dt{=}2$ to nearly opposite directions (cosine similarity $-0.70$ to $-0.94$), consistent across both Walker and Cheetah environments. Frozen controls remain near-orthogonal (cosine $\\approx 0$) and constant across all checkpoints. The full pairwise cosine similarity matrix (Figure~\\ref{fig:pairwise_heatmap}) reveals that the near-antipodal relationship between $e_{\\dt{=}1}$ and $e_{\\dt{=}2}$ is the dominant structure: most other $\\dt$-pairs remain near-orthogonal ($|\\text{cos}| < 0.2$), indicating that the RSSM selectively maximizes separation along the $\\dt{=}1$ vs.\\ $\\dt{=}2$ axis rather than learning a globally structured embedding space.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.55\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/pairwise_heatmap.png}\n\\caption{Pairwise cosine similarity between all $\\dt$-embedding vectors (exp\\_008d, seed 3, step 75K). The $e_{\\dt{=}0}$--$e_{\\dt{=}1}$ pair exhibits strong anti-correlation ($-0.70$); remaining pairs are near-orthogonal, confirming that the learned geometry is dominated by a single antipodal axis.}\n\\label{fig:pairwise_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe magnitude of the learned separation---near-antipodal rather than merely distinct---provides converging evidence that the RSSM develops maximally differentiated internal representations for the two temporal scales, which in turn may contribute to the asymmetric reward fidelity gap reported in Table~\\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity} and replicated below.\n\n\\paragraph{Extended RSSM fidelity replication (exp\\_063).}\nTo validate the fidelity gap on independently trained checkpoints, we repeat the posterior-conditioned analysis with 15 episodes $\\times$ 40 test points $\\times$ 4 $\\dt$ levels (600 samples per model) on final checkpoints from three conditions (Table~\\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity_ext}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{RSSM fidelity analysis (training-time posterior-conditioned). Reward prediction gap remains the critical differentiator; observation reconstruction is paradoxically \\emph{better} with trained embedding. Independent final-checkpoint replication (exp\\_063, 008d seed~4) yields a reward gap of $2.7\\times$, confirming the qualitative pattern at lower magnitude.}\n\\label{tab:rssm_fidelity_ext}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{obs\\_MSE}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{reward\\_MAE}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\n\\textbf{Config} & $\\dt{=}1$ & $\\dt{=}2$ & Gap & $\\dt{=}1$ & $\\dt{=}2$ & Gap \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline (009d) & 16.08 & 23.49 & 1.46$\\times$ & 0.045 & 0.059 & 1.30$\\times$ \\\\\nCollapsed (008d) & 9.96 & 13.21 & 1.33$\\times$ & 0.057 & 0.178 & 3.11$\\times$ \\\\\nFrozen (057) & 11.14 & 14.95 & 1.34$\\times$ & 0.068 & 0.133 & 1.96$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe replication confirms the original fidelity gap pattern with two additional observations. First, the collapsed model achieves the \\emph{lowest} absolute obs\\_MSE at $\\dt{=}1$ (9.96 vs.\\ baseline 16.08), indicating that the trained $\\dt$-embedding improves world model quality overall---the pathology is selective degradation of reward predictions at longer horizons, not general model failure. Second, freezing the $\\dt$-embedding reduces the reward gap from $3.11\\times$ (training-time; $2.7\\times$ in independent replication) to $1.96\\times$, providing converging evidence for its role in preventing the co-adaptation loop while leaving residual multi-step reward prediction difficulty.\n\n\\paragraph{Value prediction analysis (exp\\_064).}\nTo examine the relationship between RSSM reward fidelity and critic value estimates, we compare critic predictions $V(s)$ against actual evaluation returns across three conditions (Table~\\ref{tab:value_pred}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Critic value predictions vs.\\ actual evaluation returns. The CIVO model's critic is well-calibrated to its $\\lambda$-return targets ($V(s) \\approx$ target) but both $V$ and targets are disconnected from reality (VE${=}3.03\\times$).}\n\\label{tab:value_pred}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Config} & $V(s)$ & \\textbf{Eval} & \\textbf{VE ratio} & $V{-}\\text{target}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline (001) & 665.9 & 938.9 & 0.71$\\times$ & $+0.001$ \\\\\nCIVO (008d) & 192.7 & 63.6 & \\textbf{3.03}$\\times$ & $+0.586$ \\\\\nNo emb (008f) & 116.5 & 306.5 & 0.38$\\times$ & $+0.316$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe paradox---$V(s)$ tracks its $\\lambda$-return targets faithfully yet overestimates actual performance by $3\\times$---provides converging evidence for the hypothesized mechanism in which CIVO operates upstream of the critic: the world model generates inflated imagination trajectories that the critic learns faithfully, producing targets disconnected from reality. In the baseline, VE${<}1.0$ throughout training because $V(s)$ reflects discounted imagined returns over $H{=}15$ steps, naturally lower than undiscounted episodic evaluation. The CIVO model crosses VE${=}1.0$ at step 55K and reaches $3.03\\times$ by 105K, with the gap widening as the actor exploits the overoptimistic critic.\n\n\\paragraph{Actor entropy dynamics under CIVO.}\nExtended training of collapsed configurations reveals a distinctive temporal sequence: evaluation return collapses first, followed by actor entropy explosion ${\\sim}35$K steps later. In the most severe case (EG~v4), entropy rises from $-5.21$ to $+3.22$ nats at a rate of $+2.11$ nats/10K steps, with returns declining from 583 to 93 between steps 60K--120K. A second collapsed run (EG~v5) shows $+0.61$ nats/10K with comparable return degradation (583$\\to$102). By contrast, healthy baselines exhibit gradual entropy drift at $+0.07$ to $+0.22$ nats/10K---15--30$\\times$ slower---with returns stable above 900. The return-before-entropy temporal ordering is consistent with the causal chain: delusional critic $\\to$ bad actor gradients $\\to$ policy destabilization. The entropy explosion is a lagging indicator; the NVE diagnostic detects CIVO onset ${\\sim}35$K steps before entropy rises, consistent with its use as an early-warning signal (\\S\\ref{sec:appendix_sensitivity}).\n\n\\subsection{Selective Gradient Stopping and Gradient Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:sgs_gradient_analysis}\n\n\\subsubsection{Gradient Decomposition Results}\n\\label{sec:gradient_decomposition}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.75\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_gradient_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Gradient contribution to $e_{\\Delta t}$ by loss component at two training checkpoints. The reward head contributes the largest share (38--49\\%) yet reward-only SGS (exp\\_016e) fails; the observation decoder channel (16--22\\%) carries the critical $\\dt$-specific spatial information that drives the RSSM fidelity gap.}\n\\label{fig:gradient_decomp}\n\\end{figure}\n\nExp\\_016e collapses in the same qualitative pattern as the unmodified control: peak evaluation 326.7 at 75K steps followed by catastrophic decline to 31.7 at 95K, with NVE reaching $10.42\\times$. The SGS intervention neither prevented nor attenuated CIVO.\n\nTo explain this null result, we performed a gradient decomposition analysis using \\texttt{torch.autograd.grad} to measure each loss component's contribution to $\\nabla_\\theta e_{\\Delta t}$, where $\\theta$ denotes the temporal embedding parameters. Table~\\ref{tab:gradient_decomposition} reports the results.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Gradient contributions to the temporal embedding $e_{\\Delta t}$ by loss component, measured via \\texttt{torch.autograd.grad} with training configuration \\texttt{gate\\_fixed\\_dt=2}. Gradient norms are $\\ell_2$ norms of $\\nabla_\\theta \\mathcal{L}_i$ for each loss $\\mathcal{L}_i$.}\n\\label{tab:gradient_decomposition}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{@55K}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{@112K}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-5}\nLoss component & Share & (\\%) & Share & (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nReward head & --- & 49.0 & --- & 38.1 \\\\\nKL dynamics & --- & 34.7 & --- & 40.4 \\\\\nObservation decoders & --- & 16.3 & --- & 21.5 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe gradient decomposition reveals a counterintuitive structure: the reward head provides the largest gradient contribution (49.0\\% at 55K, 38.1\\% at 112K), yet reward-only SGS (exp\\_016e) fails catastrophically (NVE $10.42\\times$). The observation decoder channel contributes the smallest share (16.3\\% at 55K, 21.5\\% at 112K) yet carries the critical $\\dt$-specific spatial information.  This dissociation between gradient magnitude and causal importance explains why exp\\_016e, which targets the largest pathway, is functionally insufficient for CIVO prevention.\n\nThe gradient structure is relatively stable across training (reward shifts from 49\\% to 38\\%, obs from 16\\% to 22\\%, KL from 35\\% to 40\\%), unlike what a magnitude-based account would predict. The reward head's large gradient contribution reflects the scalar reward prediction task's sensitivity to temporal embedding parameters, but this gradient does not encode $\\Delta t$-specific structural information about spatial dynamics. The observation decoder channel, despite its smaller magnitude, is the only pathway that encodes how the RSSM should differentially predict multi-dimensional proprioceptive states across temporal scales.\n\n\\paragraph{Why the smallest channel is causally critical.} The observation decoder is the only loss component whose gradients encode how the RSSM should differentially predict multi-dimensional proprioceptive states ($14$ orientations, $9$ velocities, $1$ height) across temporal scales.  This $\\dt$-specific spatial information shapes the latent state representation in ways that propagate to reward prediction: the RSSM fidelity gap ($2.78\\times$ reward MAE ratio, Table~\\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}) arises because the temporal embedding learns to produce latent states that are spatially biased at $\\dt{>}1$, not because the reward head directly learns scale-dependent biases.  Full-SGS targets this causal chain by preventing observation-decoder gradients from updating $e_{\\Delta t}$, though its 0/2 CIVO elimination rate on Walker (seed~1: NVE reduced from $4.12\\times$ to $1.66\\times$ but remains CIVO-positive; seed~2: collapsed) indicates that breaking the gradient pathway alone does not fully eliminate the vulnerability.\n\n\\subsubsection{Implications for the CIVO Mechanism}\n\\label{sec:sgs_implications}\n\nThe gradient decomposition reveals that gradient magnitude and causal importance are decoupled in CIVO.\n\n\\paragraph{Gradient importance $\\neq$ gradient magnitude.} The reward head contributes the largest gradient share (38--49\\%) to $e_{\\Delta t}$, yet reward-only SGS fails.  The observation decoder contributes the smallest share (16--22\\%), yet its removal (via Full-SGS) is necessary for CIVO mitigation.  This dissociation arises because the reward head's gradient, while large, does not encode $\\dt$-specific structural information: it adjusts $e_{\\Delta t}$ to minimize a scalar prediction error that is largely $\\dt$-invariant.  The observation decoder's gradient, though smaller, encodes how the RSSM should differentially reconstruct 24-dimensional proprioceptive states across temporal scales. This is the channel through which $e_{\\Delta t}$ learns scale-dependent latent biases.\n\n\\paragraph{Reframing the intervention target.} Any gradient-level strategy that targets the reward head alone (reward prediction regularization, reward-conditioned value clipping, or reward-only SGS) targets the largest pathway but not the informationally critical one.  The failure of exp\\_016e (NVE $10.42\\times$) despite detaching the dominant gradient source confirms this.  Full-pathway SGS (exp\\_018) detaches $e_{\\Delta t}$ from all non-KL gradients, retaining only the KL dynamics contribution (34--40\\%).  However, this intervention eliminates CIVO in neither seed: seed~1 shows partial mitigation (eval 448.3, peak NVE $1.66\\times$, still CIVO-positive) while seed~2 collapses (eval 40.8). Removing the observation decoder gradient channel is thus contributory but not sufficient. Figure~\\ref{fig:civo_decomposition} decomposes evaluation returns across conditions.\n\n\\begin{figure}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.75\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_civo_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{CIVO decomposition: peak and final (105K step) evaluation returns on Walker Walk across four conditions. \\textbf{Baseline} (001): vanilla DreamerV3. \\textbf{Trained dt-emb} (008d)\\protect\\footnotemark: learned $d{=}32$ temporal embedding with multi-rate data. \\textbf{Full-SGS} (s1, s2): trained embedding with all non-KL gradients detached from $e_{\\dt}$. Trained $\\dt$-embedding degrades peak eval by $73\\%$ and final eval by $86\\%$. Full-SGS seed~1 shows partial mitigation ($-51\\%$ final, NVE $1.66\\times$, still CIVO-positive) while seed~2 collapses ($-96\\%$ final). CIVO elimination rate $= 0/2$.}\n\\label{fig:civo_decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\\footnotetext{Experiment 008d terminated at 55K steps; the 105K data point uses continuation run 009e under identical configuration.}\n\nCross-environment validation on Cheetah Run (exp\\_021) further clarifies the role of Full-SGS.  The intervention did not prevent the initial CIVO onset: NVE peaked at $9.94\\times$ at 45K (comparable to the unmitigated exp\\_010b's $8.27\\times$).  However, unlike exp\\_010b, which collapsed permanently to eval 51.7, exp\\_021 self-corrected within ${\\sim}$10K steps, with sustained NVE dropping to $0.34\\times$ (55K--105K) and final evaluation recovering to 221.5.  This transient self-correcting trajectory is not attributable to the SGS intervention: the initial CIVO onset and peak severity are comparable to the unmitigated case, and the self-correction may reflect Cheetah Run's simpler dynamics rather than SGS efficacy.  Performance remains at 38.7\\% of baseline (572.5), likely because Full-SGS over-aggressively blocks actor gradients (actor gradient norms near zero throughout training).  Combined with exp\\_018's Walker results (0/2 CIVO elimination), these findings yield a 0/3 elimination rate across environments, indicating that gradient-level interventions alone do not eliminate the CIVO vulnerability. Additional mechanisms (e.g., forward-pass conditioning effects, residual capacity-dependent instabilities) operate beyond the gradient pathway.\n\n\\paragraph{Connection to RSSM fidelity analysis.} The trained-embedding condition (exp\\_008d) shows a $2.78\\times$ reward gap between $\\Delta t{=}1$ and $\\Delta t{=}2$ transitions (Table~\\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}).  This gap arises because the observation decoder gradients shape $e_{\\Delta t}$ to produce latent states that are spatially biased at $\\dt{>}1$, and the reward head inherits these biases \\textit{through the latent state} rather than learning them directly.  The reward head's large gradient contribution reflects sensitivity to $e_{\\Delta t}$'s parameters but not causal responsibility for the fidelity gap.\n\n\\subsubsection{Entropy Dynamics Across Configurations}\n\\label{sec:entropy_dynamics}\n\n\\begin{figure}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_entropy_dynamics.pdf}\n\\caption{Actor entropy trajectories for three configurations. All start at ${\\sim}8$ nats and converge to similar values (${\\sim}{-}4.5$ to ${-}5$). The CIVO-triggering configuration (008d, red) follows a normal entropy reduction trajectory; the divergence from the control (008f) is subtle in entropy space, consistent with CIVO operating through the value channel rather than the entropy channel. 018v2 (Full-SGS, seed~1) shows slightly lower terminal entropy.}\n\\label{fig:entropy_dynamics}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsubsection{Exp\\_016e Reinterpreted as Stochastic Variation}\n\\label{sec:016e_reinterp}\n\nThe gradient decomposition establishes that exp\\_016e targets the largest gradient pathway (reward: 38--49\\%) but not the informationally critical one (observation decoders: 16--22\\%).  The apparent timing differences between exp\\_016e and exp\\_008d (later collapse onset, 75K vs.\\ 25K, and higher peak evaluation, 326.7 vs.\\ 167.2) must be attributed to stochastic variation rather than SGS effects, since the critical observation decoder channel remains intact.  All three trajectories (exp\\_008d, exp\\_009b, exp\\_016e) share the invariant identified by the NVE diagnostic: each crosses the $1.5\\times$ pathological threshold at some point during training.\n\n\\subsection{Post-CIVO Outcomes: Why Seeds Diverge}\n\\label{sec:seed_divergence}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Post-CIVO outcomes for all ten CIVO-positive runs across two environments. Peak/Final NVE measured in the 35K+ window.}\n\\label{tab:post_civo}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccl}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Env} & \\textbf{Seed} & \\textbf{Peak} & \\textbf{Final} & \\textbf{Eval} & \\textbf{Outcome} \\\\\n\\midrule\nWalker & s1 (008d) & 4.12$\\times$ & 4.12$\\times$ & 82.8 & Full collapse \\\\\nWalker & s2 (009b) & 1.62$\\times$ & 0.38$\\times$ & 267.9 & Transient CIVO \\\\\nWalker & s3 (008d\\_s3) & 1.53$\\times$ & 1.53$\\times$ & 268$^\\diamond$ & Late-onset collapse \\\\\nWalker & s4 (008d\\_s4) & 4.62$\\times$ & 4.62$\\times$ & 63.6 & Full collapse \\\\\nWalker & s5 (059) & 4.72$\\times$ & 1.22$\\times$ & 95.8 & Collapse$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCheetah & s1 (010b) & 8.27$\\times$ & 8.27$\\times$ & 51.7 & Full collapse \\\\\nCheetah & s0 (078) & 4.10$\\times$ & 0.29$\\times$ & 119.5 & Self-correcting$^\\ddagger$ \\\\\nCheetah & s0 (010b\\_s0) & 108.52$\\times$ & 108.52$\\times$ & 0.83 & Full collapse \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{1pt}\n{\\raggedright\\footnotesize $^\\diamond$ Transitional; train returns still declining at 105K. $^\\dagger$~NVE self-corrected ($4.72\\times$ $\\to$ $1.22\\times$) but policy did not recover (eval 10.5\\% of baseline). $^\\ddagger$~Peak NVE $4.10\\times$ at 75K; eval 213.7$\\to$26.1$\\to$119.5 (26.8\\% of 2-seed baseline mean, 446.1).\\par}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll ten CIVO-positive runs cross the $\\nve > 1.5\\times$ threshold (Table~\\ref{tab:post_civo}), yet the downstream damage is stochastic.\\footnote{Exp\\_009e was excluded from multi-seed analyses due to a seed configuration error (default seed=0, identical to exp\\_008d).}  Sustained collapse is the majority outcome (6/10 runs). Three runs self-correct (Walker seed~2 and both Cheetah seeds, including exp\\_078 which recovers from 26.1 to 119.5 at 105K); one (Walker seed~5) shows onset without sustained collapse---NVE self-corrects ($4.72\\times \\to 1.22\\times$) but the policy remains collapsed (eval 10.5\\% of baseline). Cheetah seed~1 (010b) exhibits more extreme overestimation (peak NVE $8.27\\times$) than Walker seeds ($1.53$--$4.62\\times$), consistent with the Cheetah reward scale amplifying the fidelity gap.  We hypothesize that the divergence relates to the critic's representational capacity at the time of CIVO onset.  \\citet{lyle2022capacity} demonstrate that deep RL networks suffer progressive capacity loss, in which early-learned features become increasingly difficult to overwrite.  If the critic's capacity is already partially consumed when CIVO injects overestimation, the network cannot recalibrate (seed~0); if sufficient capacity remains, the critic can absorb transient overestimation and recover (seed~2).\n\n\\paragraph{A fourth trajectory: CIVO-induced persistent value collapse.}\nObs-decoder SGS seed~3 (exp\\_025) exhibits a trajectory not observed in the untreated seeds: healthy through 105K ($\\nve < 1.0\\times$, eval 818.6), CIVO onset at 135K ($\\nve = 5.47\\times$, eval 67.0), followed by evaluation recovery (590.2 at 215K, 64\\% of baseline) but persistent value collapse ($\\nve = 0.14\\times$, value mean 80.6). The critic undershoots after the CIVO episode, suggesting that CIVO-induced representational damage persists even when the policy partially recovers. This contrasts with transient CIVO (seed~2, 009b), where both value and evaluation recover jointly, and with full collapse (seed~1, 008d), where neither recovers. Seed~4 exhibits the same terminal value collapse ($\\nve = 0.14\\times$ at 215K, eval 242), suggesting this overcorrection is systematic rather than seed-specific. The pattern adds a fourth post-CIVO outcome---policy recovery with critic damage---to the three documented in Table~\\ref{tab:post_civo}.\n\n\\subsubsection{Failure and Degradation Mode Taxonomy}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:failure_modes} classifies the seven distinct failure and degradation modes observed across all experimental configurations. The taxonomy reveals that CIVO is not a single failure mode but a family of related pathologies, with the trained $\\dt$-embedding creating vulnerability through at least three independent mechanisms: value-driven collapse (monotonic, late-onset, and SGS-resistant CIVO), entropy-driven instability (IRD collapse), and gradient-mediated effects (gradient starvation, SGS-paradoxical worsening).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Failure and degradation mode taxonomy. Each mode is characterized by its NVE trajectory, entropy behavior, and primary mechanism.}\n\\label{tab:failure_modes}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccl@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Mode} & \\textbf{Representative} & \\textbf{NVE} & \\textbf{Entropy} & \\textbf{Mechanism} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMonotonic CIVO & 008d, 008d\\_s4, 010b\\_s0 & $\\uparrow$ sustained & $\\downarrow$ & Reward bias $\\to$ critic $\\to$ actor \\\\\nLate-onset CIVO & 008d\\_s3 & $\\uparrow$ delayed & $\\downarrow$ & Same, delayed onset \\\\\nTransient CIVO & 009b & Peak $\\to$ $\\downarrow$ & Stable & Self-correction via capacity \\\\\nIRD collapse & 008e\\_v3\\_s2 & $\\uparrow$ late & $\\uparrow$ & Entropy-driven, values stable \\\\\nGrad.\\ starvation & 015b & Oscillates, decays & Stable & Frozen emb $\\to$ zero actor grad \\\\\nSGS-resistant & 018\\_s2 & $\\uparrow$ & $\\downarrow$ & Forward-pass conditioning \\\\\nSGS-paradoxical & 016e & $\\uparrow$ sustained & $\\downarrow$ & Reward SGS $\\to$ obs-dec reliance \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Extended Training Dynamics}\\label{sec:extended_training}\n\nOne Walker seed (s3, exp\\_045) was extended from 112K to approximately 302K steps to investigate long-horizon CIVO dynamics. After onset (NVE peak $4.91\\times$ at 132K steps), the system exhibited complete self-recovery: NVE declined to $0.05\\times$ and evaluation performance recovered to 867.6 (94.7\\% of baseline). This recovery required approximately 170K additional training steps beyond onset. The trajectory shows a single onset--recovery cycle with no recurrence, suggesting that the CIVO feedback loop can break spontaneously given sufficient training---though at substantial computational cost that the frozen $\\dt$-embedding diagnostic eliminates entirely.\n\n\\subsection{Why MPC Is Immune}\n\\label{sec:mpc_immune}\n\n\\citet{nhu2025tawm} show that $\\dt$-conditioning benefits TD-MPC2, raising the question of why it fails in DreamerV3.  The critical difference is that MPC re-plans from real states at every step, so imagination errors do not accumulate into the training signal because there is no critic bootstrapping from imagined trajectories.  In DreamerV3, the actor-critic trains entirely within imagination, and any systematic reward-prediction bias at $\\dt > 1$ is amplified through the closed actor-critic loop.\n\n\\subsection{The \\texorpdfstring{$\\dt$}{dt}-Embedding Dilemma}\n\\label{sec:embedding_dilemma}\n\nThe factorial results reveal a fundamental tension: removing the $\\dt$-embedding (008f) eliminates CIVO but at a $13\\times$ cost to reward prediction quality ($0.7469$ vs.\\ $0.0568$ MAE), as the world model cannot distinguish temporal scales.  Restricting imagination to $\\dt{=}1$ while retaining the trained embedding (008e\\_v3) avoids collapse in 2 of 4 seeds (50\\%), while the remaining two collapse via distinct mechanisms: Pathway~A (CIVO-driven, entropy decrease; s3, NVE $4.02\\times$) and Pathway~B (entropy-driven, entropy increase; s2, NVE $3.78\\times$; \\S\\ref{sec:f6}).  Neither approach alone provides a complete solution.\n\n\\subsection{Temporal Embedding Representation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:cosine_evolution}\n\nTo understand how the trained $\\dt$-embedding creates vulnerability, we analyze the cosine similarity between $e(\\dt{=}1)$ and $e(\\dt{=}2)$ across training (exp\\_065). Trained embeddings learn \\emph{near-opposite} representations: $\\cos(e_1, e_2) = {-}0.87$ to ${-}0.94$ at 25K steps across three configurations (Walker 008d\\_s3, Cheetah 010b\\_s0, Walker 059\\_s5), persisting at ${-}0.70$ to ${-}0.86$ at 75K steps. In contrast, frozen embeddings maintain near-zero similarity ($-0.15$ to $+0.07$, constant throughout training). The near-antipodal learned representations explain the large reward prediction gap (\\S\\ref{sec:f2}): $\\dt{=}2$ conditioning pushes the RSSM into a qualitatively different region of latent space, producing systematically biased predictions rather than minor perturbations.\n\n\\subsection{\\texorpdfstring{$\\dt$}{dt}-Embedding Differentiation Precedes Value Divergence}\n\\label{sec:cosine_temporal}\n\nThe preceding analysis established that trained $\\dt$-embeddings learn near-antipodal representations. Here we resolve the \\emph{temporal dynamics} of this differentiation across 11 experiments (${\\sim}$47 checkpoints), testing whether embedding differentiation is a consequence or precursor of CIVO.\n\n\\paragraph{Trained embeddings: monotonic relaxation.}\nAcross all trained configurations, $\\cos(e_1, e_2)$ starts near $-0.95$ within 10K steps and relaxes monotonically toward ${\\sim}{-}0.65$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cosine_evolution_ext}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cosine similarity evolution between $e(\\dt{=}1)$ and $e(\\dt{=}2)$ for trained $\\dt$-embeddings. Relaxation is monotonic but never reaches zero; frozen embeddings remain constant near zero throughout training.}\n\\label{tab:cosine_evolution_ext}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Env} & \\textbf{Experiment} & \\textbf{10K} & \\textbf{25K} & \\textbf{65K} & \\textbf{75K} & \\textbf{302K} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{Walker} & exp\\_066b (trained) & $-0.947$ & $-0.928$ & $-0.847$ & --- & --- \\\\\n & exp\\_045 (extension) & --- & --- & --- & --- & $-0.668$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCheetah & exp\\_010b$\\to$048 & --- & $-0.904$ & --- & $-0.841$ & $-0.639$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textit{Frozen controls:}} \\\\\nWalker & exp\\_057 (frozen) & \\multicolumn{5}{c}{$-0.150$ (constant, 25K--225K)} \\\\\nCheetah & exp\\_058 (frozen) & \\multicolumn{5}{c}{$+0.069$ (constant, 25K--225K)} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nCross-seed consistency at 25K steps is high: mean $\\cos = -0.910 \\pm 0.028$ ($n{=}5$ Walker seeds), confirming the relaxation pattern is robust across random seeds. Variance increases with training ($-0.789 \\pm 0.076$ at 75K), reflecting seed-dependent relaxation rates.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal precedence over CIVO.}\nExp\\_066b provides simultaneous cosine similarity and CIVO diagnostic data at 5K resolution (Table~\\ref{tab:cosine_vs_civo}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cosine similarity relaxation and CIVO diagnostic for exp\\_066b.
